Golub and Van Loan 2013). Finally, the detection of a single\\r\\nnew leak occurring in the WDN, along with its starting time, may\\r\\nbe accomplished by applying change point detection (Step LI-3) to\\r\\nthe time series kMREsðtÞkF. After a leak is definitively identified\\r\\nand becomes distinct, the model may be recalibrated to be reset for\\r\\nnew leakage identification.\\r\\nMultiple simultaneous leaks will also be reflected in the time\\r\\nseries kMREsðtÞkF in an aggregated form [compare Eq. (13)].\\r\\nHowever, disaggregation into individual leaks is not straightfor-\\r\\nward in the current implementation of LILA. Thus, for a more pre-\\r\\ncise analysis of each individual leak within the overall aggregated\\r\\nerror, we manually selected, for each leak, an individual combina-\\r\\ntion of nodes that revealed the leak signal in the most distinct\\r\\nfashion.\\r\\nLI-3: Change Point Detection\\r\\nThe last task of leakage identification is the detection of variations\\r\\nin the MRE time series induced by a leak flow; therefore, this rep-\\r\\nresents a change point detection problem. Change point detection\\r\\nproblems have been widely studied in the literature, and there is a\\r\\nmultitude of statistical methods available. For a comprehensive\\r\\nreview we refer the reader to Aminikhanghahi and Cook (2017).\\r\\nAs explained in the previous section, we expect the MRE to be\\r\\nunbiased and normally distributed for the network state without\\r\\nleaks. With the start of a leak event, the error mean will either\\r\\nabruptly or gradually shift away from zero, depending on the nature\\r\\nof the leak. Successful change point detection aims to flag these\\r\\ninstances as early as possible, while at the same time avoiding false\\r\\nalarms. In LILA, we apply the widely used Cusum algorithm\\r\\n(Montgomery 2020), known from statistical process monitoring,\\r\\nto automatically detect changes in the MRE.\\r\\nThe Cusum algorithm is a method to detect changes in the prob-\\r\\nability distribution of an univariate signal. It accumulates the dis-\\r\\ntance Ci of the target variable x from its mean μx, corrected by a\\r\\nslack value K, which is chosen based on the change of mean that is\\r\\nto be detected. Often K is selected based on the estimated standard\\r\\ndeviation σ\\u0003x of the target variable multiplied by a parameter δ\\r\\n[cf. Eq. (16)]. A change point is flagged once Ci exceeds a prede-\\r\\nfined threshold H ¼ η × σx. In general, the algorithm comes in two\\r\\nforms for detecting positive and negative changes from the mean.\\r\\nFor our application, we use the formulation to detect changes in the\\r\\npositive direction because leaks will manifest themselves as posi-\\r\\ntive values in the MRE (cf. section LI-2: Reconstruction Error\\r\\nAnalysis)\\r\\nCþ\\r\\ni ¼ max½0; xi −ðμ0 þ KÞ þ Cþ\\r\\ni−1\\u0002\\r\\nwith\\r\\nCþ\\r\\n0 ¼ 0\\r\\nand\\r\\nK ¼ δ\\r\\n2 · σ\\u0003x\\r\\nð16Þ\\r\\nTo evaluate the general suitability of the Cusum method for the\\r\\nleak identification problem, we created artificial error trajectories\\r\\nby adding different levels of white noise to given ground truth leak\\r\\ntrajectories. This allowed us to confirm the algorithm’s ability to\\r\\nrobustly flag the leaks and to select the hyperparameters in a rea-\\r\\nsonable range (cf. Appendix). Moreover, we can derive some useful\\r\\nintuitions. Naturally, the change point method performs better for\\r\\nhigher ratios of leak magnitude to signal noise. This reflects the fact\\r\\nthat a smaller model error and better signal quality enable earlier\\r\\nleak detection. The same holds for the temporal development of the\\r\\nleak, as abrupt bursts are easier to detect than slow transients.\\r\\nLeakage Localization\\r\\nThe leakage localization step has three subcomponents, including\\r\\nwater demand calibration, pressure difference analysis to identify\\r\\nthe pipes with the highest influence on the pressure sensors, and the\\r\\napplication of the SLA method developed by Berglund et al. (2017)\\r\\nto quantify the approximate leakage magnitude and localize the\\r\\nleak in the WDN. The three steps shown in Fig. 1 work sequentially\\r\\nto, first, partially calibrate the hydraulic model using demand data.\\r\\nSecond, a pressure difference analysis is conducted to define the\\r\\norder of the pipes that affect each pressure sensor of the water net-\\r\\nwork and report them as leak candidates. Third, the application of\\r\\nSLA aims at finding the magnitude of a leak from the candidates\\r\\nthat could be producing the discrepancy between nonleak and leak\\r\\npressure values. A detailed explanation of each leak localization\\r\\ncomponent is presented in what follows.\\r\\nLL-1: Water Demand Calibration\\r\\nWater demand calibration aims at matching the simulated and the\\r\\nobserved demand to replicate real conditions with the hydraulic\\r\\nmodel (Walski 2017). LILA requires a demand-calibrated model\\r\\nto replicate the demand and the pressure measured values reported\\r\\nby the sensors. To calibrate the demand patterns and base demand\\r\\nin the WDN hydraulic model, we use automatic meter reading\\r\\n(AMR) data available for the simulation period. While LILA outperforms the BattLe-\\r\\nDIM benchmark, there are events where the localization step does\\r\\nnot improve the location reported by the BattLeDIM benchmark\\r\\nbecause of the noise in the data and the difference between the\\r\\ndetected and the true start time of the leaks. For personal use only; all rights reserved.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n Wang PDF PAGE 3 \\r\\n that DMA flow is more sensitive to leakage events, and pressure\\r\\nsensors are more widely distributed in the observation field than\\r\\ninlet flowmeters. To facilitate the identification of leak-induced\\r\\ndata from the time series monitoring data, the anomalies caused by\\r\\nthe leakage need to be highlighted.\\r\\nDMA inflow has received wide attention in leak detection given\\r\\nits sensitivity for abnormal leak states. Leakage is the extra con-\\r\\nsumption apart from normal user consumption of DMA, whose es-\\r\\ntimation is undertaken by calculating the deviation between DMA\\r\\ninflow and user consumption. Therefore, the estimation of DMA\\r\\ninflow, or referred to as DMA water consumption under normal\\r\\nconditions, is the first procedure for leakage detection and isolation.\\r\\nThis section proposes a joint estimation method based on two\\r\\nDMAs in the same city, one nonleakage DMA-a is used to extract\\r\\nthe seasonal trend of this city, and one unidentified DMA-b is used\\r\\nto extract the weekly water consumption in a nonleaking state.\\r\\nBased on seasonal and weekly changes, the normal water consump-\\r\\ntion of DMA-b can be estimated.\\r\\nFor DMA-a, water consumption is a typical nonstationary time\\r\\nseries. Previous studies have shown that empirical mode decompo-\\r\\nsition (EMD) is an efficient tool for analyzing nonlinear and non-\\r\\nstationary data (Huang et al. 1998; Karthikeyan and Nagesh Kumar\\r\\n2013). In this study, EMD is proposed to extract the seasonal trend\\r\\nof normal water consumption. The authors assume that the water\\r\\nconsumption data are composed of trend items (changes within the\\r\\nseason), periodic items (changes within the week), and random\\r\\nitems (by stochastic factors), given as\\r\\nYðtÞ ¼ SðtÞ × TðtÞ × RðtÞ\\r\\nð1Þ\\r\\nwhere YðtÞ = normal water consumption of DMA-a; SðtÞ = trend\\r\\nterm; TðtÞ = periodic term; and RðtÞ = random term in the data.\\r\\nIt should be noted that Eq. (1) needs to be logarithmically trans-\\r\\nformed before EMD. The residual term of the raw signal after EMD\\r\\nrepresents the seasonal trend of water consumption.\\r\\nFor DMA-b, the nonleakage week pattern can be extracted by\\r\\nthe demand pattern of the hydraulic model or the average of multi-\\r\\nple nonleakage weekly demands. Based on seasonal and weekly\\r\\nchanges, the estimated normal water consumption in the DMA-b\\r\\ncan be calculated by the following method, given as\\r\\nDemandDMA−b ¼ SðtÞ × qðtÞ\\r\\nð2Þ\\r\\nwhere qðtÞ = total demand of DMA at time t. We can get the weekly\\r\\nthrough the flow of DMA-b when there is no leakage, which also\\r\\nis a water use pattern in a week. qðtÞ is obtained by repeatedly\\r\\nextending the pattern to one year.\\r\\nAnother indicator that responds to leakage is pressure. Different\\r\\nfrom the flowmeters located at the entrance and exit of DMA, there\\r\\nare generally more correlated pressure sensors in the same DMA\\r\\narea (Wu and Liu 2017a; Xu et al. 2020). When a leak occurs in the\\r\\npipe network, the pressure at the node adjacent to the leak will de-\\r\\ncrease, and the closer the node is to the leak, the greater the pressure\\r\\ndrop. Similarly, when the leak is repaired, the closer the node is\\r\\nto the leak, the greater the pressure rise. Therefore, the pressure\\r\\nincrease or decrease, that is, the pressure trend at each node, is used\\r\\nas the highlight indicator of the leakage in this section.\\r\\nThe seasonal-trend decomposition procedure based on Loess\\r\\n(STL) is proposed to estimate the pressure trend from ordinary\\r\\npressure time series (Cleveland et al. 1990). The pressure time\\r\\nseries P is decomposed into three series including daily compo-\\r\\nnent D, trend component T, and residual component R, given as\\r\\nPiðtÞ ¼ DiðtÞ þ TiðtÞ þ RiðtÞ\\r\\nð3Þ\\r\\nwhere TiðtÞ = pressure trend time series at pressure sensor i in\\r\\nthe DMA-b.\\r\\nIdentification of Leakages\\r\\nLeakage is the extra consumption apart from the normal water\\r\\nconsumption in DMA. When a leak occurs, the SCADA system\\r\\nwill record online monitoring data containing the leak. The flow\\r\\nand pressure caused by the leakage are highlighted in the previous\\r\\n“Estimation of Flow and Pressure” section as (1) the flow residual\\r\\nbetween normal water consumption and the actual water consump-\\r\\ntion monitored by SCADA sensors; and (2) the pressure trend at\\r\\ndifferent nodes. In this section, the authors use the presented two\\r\\nfeatures to focus on solving two identification problems: (1) the\\r\\ndetection of leaks; and (2) the assessment of leaks.\\r\\nThe purpose of leak detection is to identify the start time, end\\r\\ntime, and volume of leaks and to provide zonal (or DMA-level) leak\\r\\ntips for the water utility. Previous studies have suggested that leak-\\r\\nage is more sensitive to flow than to pressure (Mounce et al. 2011;\\r\\nYe and Fenner 2011). Considering the timeliness and accuracy of\\r\\nleak identification, the flow residual series between normal water\\r\\nconsumption and the actual water consumption monitored by\\r\\nSCADA sensors is used for solving the first leak detection problem.\\r\\nFor flow residual data, the characteristics of the flow residual\\r\\nsequence should be analyzed first. Although the residuals series is\\r\\ncalculated by the difference between observed and estimated series,\\r\\nit is still a time series with irregular fluctuations. To reduce the non-\\r\\nstationary residual series and amplify the leakage signal implicit\\r\\nin the residual series, a multiscale difference method is proposed\\r\\nin this study. Difference operation, also known as finite difference,\\r\\ndifference function, or difference operation, is a concept in math-\\r\\nematics. It maps the original function fðxÞ to fðx þ aÞ −fðx þ bÞ.\\r\\nDifference operation, corresponding to differential operation, is an\\r\\nimportant concept in calculus, which is commonly used for the\\r\\nsmoothing of nonstationary time series (Flajolet and Sedgewick\\r\\n1995). In this study, in order to eliminate the change of the mean\\r\\nvalue and highlight the mutation of the sequence over time, the\\r\\ndifference value ΔrðxkÞ of the continuous items in the residual\\r\\nsequence is calculated by Eqs. Baseline Comparison on Benchmark WDNs\\r\\nIn this set of experiments we compared the performance of GATRes‐small against two state‐of‐the‐art baseline\\r\\nmodels, GraphConvWat (Hajgató et al., 2021a, 2021b) and mGCN (Ashraf et al., 2023). We evaluated the three\\r\\nmodels on four benchmark WDNs: Anytown, C‐Town, Richmond, and L‐Town, described in Section 5.1. The\\r\\nexperiments were conducted following the method proposed by Hajgató\\r\\net al. (2020) to ensure a fair comparison. Specifically, 1,000, 10,000, and\\r\\n20,000 snapshots were generated for the C‐Town, L‐Town, and Richmond\\r\\nWDNs, respectively. Then, the data sets were split into training, validation,\\r\\nand test sets in a 6:2:2 ratio.\\r\\nWe used the same experimental settings as proposed in the baseline ap-\\r\\nproaches to guarantee a fair comparison. Thus, in all experiments the models\\r\\nare trained for 2,000 epochs with early stopping, using the Adam gradient‐\\r\\nbased optimization algorithm (Kingma & Ba, 2014). The GraphConvWat\\r\\nmodel training was stopped if the validation loss did not improve for 50\\r\\nconsecutive epochs. In the case of mGCN, the training was stopped if no\\r\\nimprovement is seen after 250 epochs. Likewise mGCN, our model training is\\r\\nstopped after 250 epochs if no improvement is observed. In all cases, it is\\r\\nconsidered an improvement when the validation loss decreases at least\\r\\nby 1e−6.\\r\\nFigure 8. Likewise,\\r\\nGATRes‐small achieves the higher NSE in all WDNs but Richmond.\\r\\nOne limitation of previous approaches is the evaluation of model performance\\r\\non unrealistic data, that is, an exact copy of the training data distribution (Section 2.2). In previous approaches, the\\r\\nsnapshots representing random WDN states used for training, validation and test are created by the same algo-\\r\\nrithm. Consequently, the distribution of the data used for testing is a fidelity copy of the data used for training.\\r\\nHowever, in practice, the distribution of the real data differs from the data used for training the reconstruction\\r\\nmodels, as explained in Section 2.2. Therefore, it is important that the models adapt to circumvent such un-\\r\\ncertainties. Previous approaches achieve impressive performance when tested on replicas of the training data (see\\r\\nTable 11), but the performance drop is evident when they are evaluated on a realistic scenario (see Table 10).\\r\\nThe density distributions of training and test sets in C‐Town, Richmond, and L‐Town WDNs are shown in\\r\\nFigure 9. It is clear that the distributions of the training and testing data created by the same algorithm (math-\\r\\nematical simulation) are identical, while the distribution of the test data with a demand pattern greatly differs from\\r\\nthe one used during training. This shows the ability of GATRes to adapt to the changes that occur in real life\\r\\nscenarios. It also shows that other models achieve better results only when evaluated on fidelity copies of the\\r\\ntraining data, caused by overfitting due to the large model complexity of the previous approaches. The density\\r\\ndistribution plot in Figure 9b explains the good performance of mGCN on Richmond WDN in terms of MAPE\\r\\nand NSE (Table 10), the time‐based demand pattern test data set has a similar distribution than the one used for\\r\\ntraining.\\r\\n6.5. Ablation Study\\r\\nThe ablation study presented in this section evaluates the importance of the different components of GATRes\\r\\nmodel architecture, and the effect of their removal or alteration on performance. In every run, a specific\\r\\ncomponent is removed or altered, and GATRes is restored to its original version before a new change is made. import pandas as pd
import numpy as np


def global_preprocessing(X_data, leak_labels, time_start, time_win_len, sensors_idx=None):
    X = []
    Y = []
    y_leak = []
    
    if sensors_idx is None:
        sensors_idx = list(range(X_data.shape[1]))
    
     # Use a sliding time window to construct a labeled data set
    t_index = time_start
    time_points = range(len(leak_labels))
    i = 0
    while t_index < len(time_points) - time_win_len:
        # Grab time window from data stream
        x = X_data[t_index:t_index+time_win_len-1, sensors_idx]

        #######################
        # Feature engineering #
        #######################
        x = np.mean(x,axis=0)  # "Stupid" feature
        X.append(x)

        Y.append([X_data[t_index + time_win_len-1, n] for n in sensors_idx])

        y_leak.append(leak_labels[t_index + time_win_len-1])

        t_index += 1  # Note: Overlapping time windows
        i += 1

    X = np.array(X)
    Y = np.array(Y)
    y_leak = np.array(y_leak)

    return X, Y, y_leak

def load_trajectories_true(path,max_norm=False):
    df_leak_ts = pd.DataFrame()
    
    # load csv from data folder
    df_leak_ts_ = pd.read_csv(path,
                             delimiter=';',
                             parse_dates=['Timestamp'], 
                             index_col='Timestamp',
                              decimal=',')

    df_leak_ts = pd.concat([df_leak_ts, df_leak_ts_], axis=0).fillna(0)
        
            
    # get leak start and end dates
    df_leak_start = df_leak_ts.ne(0).idxmax().sort_values()
    df_leak_end = df_leak_ts.iloc[::-1].ne(0).idxmax()  
    
    # organize leaks in temporal order
    df_leak_ts = df_leak_ts[df_leak_start.sort_values().index]
    
    # normalize trajectories by max-leak-size for leak-type-assignment
    df_leak_max = df_leak_ts.max()
    df_leak_ts = df_leak_ts/df_leak_max
                
    # create df_leak_overview
    lst_index=['leak_type', 'leak_start', 'leak_fix', 'delta_t_expert']
    df_leak_overview = pd.DataFrame(index=lst_index,
                                    columns=df_leak_ts.columns)
    
    # determin leak-type based on first leak instance
    lst_leak_type = []
    for i, t_start in enumerate(df_leak_start):
        first_leak_value = df_leak_ts.loc[t_start, df_leak_start.index[i]]
    
        if first_leak_value>0.95:
            lst_leak_type.append('abrupt')
        if first_leak_value<0.05:
            lst_leak_type.append('incipient')
            
    df_leak_overview.loc['leak_type'] = lst_leak_type
        
    for leak in df_leak_overview:
        df_leak_overview.loc['leak_start', leak] = pd.to_datetime(df_leak_start[leak])
        df_leak_overview.loc['leak_fix', leak] = pd.to_datetime(df_leak_end[leak]) 
    
    # labels from PERFECT_TEAM solution
    dct_true_start = dict({'p523': '2019-01-15 23:00',
                           'p827': '2019-01-24 18:30',
                           'p280': '2019-02-10 13:05', #DMA C
                           'p653': '2019-03-03 13:10',
                           'p710': '2019-03-24 14:15',
                           'p514': '2019-04-02 20:40',
                           'p331': '2019-04-20 10:10',
                           'p193': '2019-05-19 10:40',
                           'p277': '2019-05-30 21:55', #DMA C
                           'p142': '2019-06-12 19:55',
                           'p680': '2019-07-10 08:45',
                           'p586': '2019-07-26 14:40',
                           'p721': '2019-08-02 03:00',
                           'p800': '2019-08-16 14:00',
                           'p123' :'2019-09-13 20:05',
                           'p455': '2019-10-03 14:00',
                           'p762': '2019-10-09 10:15',
                           'p426': '2019-10-25 13:25',
                           'p879': '2019-11-20 11:55'})
    
    # overwrite trajectory starts by given times
    for pipe in dct_true_start.keys():
        df_leak_overview.loc['leak_start', pipe] = pd.Timestamp(dct_true_start[pipe])
    
    # re-scale df_leak_ts if max-normalization not desired
    if max_norm==False:
        df_leak_ts = df_leak_ts*df_leak_max

    dct_expert_annotations = dict({'p523': '2019-01-15 23:00',

                                   'p280': '2019-02-10 13:40', #DMA C

This indicates that for high-elevation nodes experiencing data scarcity, extending the time series length and incorporating more prior temporal information enhances forecast accuracy. Conversely, for nodes in data-rich areas, additional temporal information may introduce unnecessary noise or redundancy, reducing model accuracy. This finding suggests that, for nodes in data-scarce conditions, incorporating more prior information and adopting more suitable data fusion techniques will be key to enhancing the model’s performance.\\n\\n### 4.2. import sys
import os
import numpy as np
import pandas as pd
from sklearn.metrics import mean_squared_error

from LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing import global_preprocessing
from MyModel import MyModel, create_model
from FaultDetection import EnsembleSystem
from utils import evaluate_fault_detection
from models import KNN, LinearModel
from SAM_KNN_Regression import SAMKNNRegressor


path_to_data = "data/"

time_win_len = 4  # Always consider the last four time steps
time_start = 100  # Ignore the first 100 samples

t_train_split = 3000    # Use the first 3000 sampels for training -- there are no anomalies present early until end of January!  
batch_size = 200  # Number of samples used for adapting the model to the fault

        The incomplete pressure signal matrix w/ 'n' number of observations, for n timesteps
        This is the feature vector (x) for the GNN model
        
        x =
        [21.57, 22.81, 23.13, ... , t-n    <- n1, pressure at node 1 is observed
         0.0  , 0.0  , 0.0  , ... , t-n    <- n2, pressure at node 2 is unknown
         0.0  , 0.0  , 0.0  , ... , t-n    <- n3, ... unknown
         22.43, 22.51, 23.41, ... , t-n    <- n4, ... observed
         0.0  , 0.0  , 0.0  , ... , t-n    <- n5, ... unknown
         ...     ]   etc.
        
    y : np.array(n_obs,n_nodes,2)
        The complete pressure signal matrix w/ 'n' number of observations.
        With this we may train the GNN in a supervised manner.
        
        y =
        [21.57    <- n1, all values are observed
         21.89    <- n2,
         22.17    <- n3
         22.43    <- n4
         23.79    <- n5
         ...  ]   etc.
        
    '''
    # The number of nodes in the passed dataframe
    n_nodes = len(pressure_df.columns)
    
    # Rename the columns (n1, n2, ...) to numerical values (1, 2, ...)
    pressure_df.columns = [number for number in range(1,n_nodes+1)]
    
    # Perform scaling on the initial Pandas Dataframe for brevity
    # This is less trivial than applying it on the later generated numpy arrays
    
    # Standard scale:
    if rescale == 'standard':
        _avg        = pressure_df.stack().mean()        # Calc. avg. over entire df.
        _std        = pressure_df.stack().std(ddof=0)   # Calc. std.. over entire df.
        bias        = _avg                              # Avg. is the scaling bias
        scale       = _std                              # Std.dev. is the scaling range
        pressure_df = (pressure_df - bias) / scale      # Scale to range
        
    # Min/max scaling (normalising):
    elif rescale == 'minmax':
        _min        = min(pressure_df.min())            # Find the absolute minimum value
        _max        = max(pressure_df.max())            # Find the absolute maximum value
        _rng        = _max - _min                       # Calculate the difference between (range)
        bias        = _min                              # Scaling bias is the min value
        scale       = _rng                              # Scaling range is the min-max range
        pressure_df = (pressure_df - bias) / scale      # Scale to range
        
    # Perform no scaling
    else:
        bias        = None
        scale       = None
    
    # DataFrame where the index is the node number holding the sensor and the value is set to 1
    sensor_df = pd.DataFrame(data=[1 for i in observed_nodes],index=observed_nodes)
    
    # Filled single row of DataFrame with the complete number of nodes, the unmonitored nodes are set to 0
    sensor_df = sensor_df.reindex(list(range(1,n_nodes+1)),fill_value=0)
    
    # Find the number of rows in the DataFrame to be masked...
    n_rows = len(pressure_df)
    
    # ... and complete a mask DataFrame, where all the observations to keep are set to 1 and the rest to 0
    mask_df = sensor_df.T.append([sensor_df.T for i in range(n_rows-1)],ignore_index=True)
    
    # Enforce matching indices of the two DataFrames to be broadcast together
    mask_df.index = pressure_df.index
    
    
    scenario_id = sys.argv[1]
    model_desc = sys.argv[2]
    use_faulty_sensor_scenario = True if sys.argv[3] == "True" else False

    # Load data
    X, y, nodes, pressure_nodes, flow_nodes, leaky_pipe, faulty_sensor = load_data(scenario_id, use_faulty_sensor_scenario)

    # Preprocessing
    X_all, Y_all, leak_labels_all = global_preprocessing(X, y, time_start, time_win_len, list(range(len(pressure_nodes))))  
    n_dim = X_all.shape[1]

    X_all_train, X_all_test = X_all[:t_train_split,:], X_all[t_train_split:,:]
    Y_all_train, Y_all_test = Y_all[:t_train_split,:], Y_all[t_train_split:,:]
    fault_labels_all_train, fault_labels_all_test = leak_labels_all[:t_train_split], leak_labels_all[t_train_split:]

    faulty_times = np.where(fault_labels_all_test == 1)[0]
    test_t0, test_t1= faulty_times[0], faulty_times[-1] + 50  # Time period in the test set where a fault is present + some a time buffer for the original hydraulics to recover from the fault

    # Fit model
    def get_model():
        if model_desc == "knn":
            return KNN(n_dim-1, k=5, window_size=500)
        elif model_desc == "linear":
            return LinearModel(n_dim-1)
        elif model_desc == "samknn":
            return SAMKNNRegressor(multi_dim_y=False, max_LTM_size=100, n_neighbors=5, error_type="MAE")

    my_model_gen = lambda: create_model(get_model())
    model = EnsembleSystem(my_model_gen, flow_nodes, pressure_nodes)
    #model = EnsembleSystem(MyModel, flow_nodes, pressure_nodes)
    model.fit(X_all_train, Y_all_train)

    # Online learning -- test if anomalies are found
    suspicous_time_points = []
    t = 0
    while t + batch_size < X_all_test.shape[0]:
        X_batch, Y_batch = X_all_test[t:t+batch_size,:], Y_all_test[t:t+batch_size,:]

        # Apply anomaly detection to current batch
        time_points, _ = model.apply_detector(X_batch, Y_batch)
        for tp in time_points:
            suspicous_time_points.append(t + tp)

        # Adapt to current batch
        model.fit(X_batch, Y_batch)

        # Next batch
        t += batch_size

"""Check documentation links, archived hashes and completed audit evidence; no training."""
import argparse
import json
from pathlib import Path
import re
import sys
from urllib.parse import unquote

ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]
sys.path.insert(0, str(ROOT))
from repro.data import digest


def main():
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__)
    parser.add_argument('--reproduction-dir', type=Path,
                        help='Optional fresh reproduction to compare with committed numeric outputs.')
    args = parser.parse_args()
    broken, links = [], 0
    for path in [ROOT / 'README.md', *sorted((ROOT / 'docs').glob('*.md'))]:
        for url in re.findall(r'\[[^\]]*\]\(([^\s]+?)\)', path.read_text(encoding='utf-8')):
            if url.startswith(('https://', 'http://', '#', 'mailto:')):
                continue
            links += 1
            target = path.parent / unquote(url.split('#')[0].strip('<>'))
            if not target.exists():
                broken.append(f'{path.relative_to(ROOT)}: {url}')
    if broken:
        raise AssertionError(broken)
    historical = json.loads((ROOT / 'provenance/historical_artifacts.json').read_text())
    for item in historical['artifacts']:
        assert digest(ROOT / item['file']) == item['sha256'], item['file']
    instructions = json.loads((ROOT / 'provenance/engineering_prompt/v2_1_verified_instructions.json').read_text(encoding='utf-8'))
    assert digest(ROOT / 'provenance/engineering_prompt/v2_original_as_received.json') == instructions['audit_corrections']['source_sha256']
    geometry_dir = ROOT / 'results/engineering'
    manifest = json.loads((geometry_dir / 'audit_manifest.json').read_text())
    for group in ['code_sha256', 'protocol_sha256']:
        for name, expected in manifest[group].items():
            assert digest(ROOT / name) == expected, name
    for name, expected in manifest['output_sha256'].items():
        assert digest(geometry_dir / name) == expected, name
    archived_run = json.loads((ROOT / 'results/reproduced/run_manifest.json').read_text())
    for name, expected in archived_run['code_sha256'].items():
        assert digest(ROOT / name) == expected, name
    available, omitted = [], []
    for name, expected in archived_run['output_sha256'].items():
        path = ROOT / 'results/reproduced' / name
        if path.exists():
            assert digest(path) == expected, name
            available.append(name)
        else:
            # Full derived tables are intentionally Git-ignored and may be absent in a clone.
            assert name in {'selected_inputs_full.csv.gz', 'forecast_pairs_full.csv.gz'}, name
            omitted.append(name)
    notebook = json.loads((ROOT / 'notebooks/01_milestone2_evidence.ipynb').read_text(encoding='utf-8'))
    cells = [c for c in notebook['cells'] if c['cell_type'] == 'code']
    assert all(c.get('execution_count') is not None for c in cells)
    assert not any(o['output_type'] == 'error' for c in cells for o in c.get('outputs', []))
    compared = []
    if args.reproduction_dir:
        for name in ['eda_statistics.json', 'correlations.csv', 'topology_audit.json',
                     'screening_summary.json', 'table_checks.json', 'verification.json']:
            assert digest(args.reproduction_dir / name) == digest(ROOT / 'results/reproduced' / name), name
            compared.append(name)
    print(json.dumps({'status': 'PASS', 'local_markdown_links_checked': links,
                      'broken_links': broken, 'historical_artifacts_hash_checked': len(historical['artifacts']),
                      'archived_output_hashes_checked': len(available), 'ignored_outputs_absent': omitted,
                      'engineering_manifest_matches': True, 'prompt_archive_hash_matches': True,
                      'notebook_code_cells_inspected': len(cells), 'notebook_cell_errors': 0,
                      'notebook_reexecuted_by_this_check': False,
                      'fresh_numeric_outputs_byte_identical': compared,
                      'models_trained': False}, indent=2))


if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()

import numpy as np
from sklearn.metrics import r2_score, mean_squared_error, mean_absolute_error

from SAM_KNN_Regression import SAMKNNRegressor


class MyModel():
    def __init__(self, model=SAMKNNRegressor(multi_dim_y=False, max_LTM_size=100, n_neighbors=5, error_type="MAE")):
        self.model = model
    
    def fit(self, X, y):
        self.model.partial_fit(X, y)
    
    def predict(self, X):
        return self.model.predict(X)
    
    def score(self, X, y):
        y_pred = self.predict(X)
        return r2_score(y, y_pred)
        #return mean_squared_error(y, y_pred)
        #return mean_absolute_error(y, y_pred)
    
    def get_stat_abs_deviation(self, X, y):
        y_pred = self.predict(X)
        difference_abs = np.abs(y_pred - y)
        
        return np.max(difference_abs), np.min(difference_abs), np.mean(difference_abs)


def create_model(model):
    return MyModel(model)

Explainable forecasting framework\n\nThe GRU [6] is a highly optimized variant of the recurrent neural network. Its core advantage lies in its unique architectural design. Through the precise regulatory mechanisms of the update gate and reset gate, the GRU skillfully balances the retention and forgetting of historical information [7]. This allows it to capture the dynamics of long-term and short-term dependencies in time series data [24]. The GGNN, an innovative extension of the GRU [17], integrates recurrent layers, graph convolution, and gating mechanisms. However, the GGNN also faces limitations of the convolution-based graph methods: Each iteration only focuses on the first-order neighborhood of the sensor nodes. Garðar Örn Garðarsson  et al. / IFAC PapersOnLine 55-6 (2022) 661–666
Fig. 3. ChebNet model for graph signal reconstruction.
The predictor (Fig. 1) has the same structure but the
input channel chin, has a depth of 3 time periods.
has a similar number of junctions and pipes as the WDN
we benchmark our model on. The model consists solely of
Chebyshev convolutional layers, which are regularised by
the weight decay of the gradient descent optimiser. Layer
weights are initialised by the Xavier normal distribution
(Glorot and Bengio (2010)) and biases set to zero. The
hidden layers are activated with a SiLU sigmoid linear
unit (Hendrycks and Gimpel (2016)) and the output layer
is passed through a sigmoid activation. The ADAM opti-
miser (Kingma and Ba (2015)) is used due to its properties
of having an adaptive learning rate with momentum, that
adjusts to the function to be optimised.
An exhaustive ablation study to optimise hyper parame-
ters was provided by Hajgató et al. (2021) leading to the
following architecture for the WDN of Richmond: 4 Cheby-
shev spectral graph convolutional layers with the degree of
the polynomial in the hidden layers set to [K1, K2, K3, ] =
[240, 120, 20], and filter sizes [F1, F2, F3] = [120, 60, 30].
This forms the basis of our architecture, depicted in Fig. 3.
The input channel size, chin, here refers to the depth of
the input; in the case of the reconstructor, this will then
equal one, as a single input graph is used to generate the
output, whereas for the predictor, this will be equivalent
to the T number of time step used to generate the out-
put prediction. From this basis, we optimised the graph
construction methodologies (weights assignment, signal
scaling methodologies, and with or without self-loop for
observed nodes) as motivated in Sec. 2.1, and the window
size for the predictor.
Detection and Localisation Methodology
We now for-
malise the proposed method with Algorithm 1 and focus
on i) the estimation error, ii) the conversion from node
to edge error (or residual), iii) fault detection and leakage
localisation.
Node Estimation Error Evaluation
The outputs of the
predictor and reconstructor are compared, by evaluating
at each iteration the difference between the reconstructed
pressure at time t with the predicted one, which was
generated at t −1 using past information. The difference
is the prediction error or residual signal, namely r(t) =
[r1(t), r2(t), . . . , rN(t)] = ˆy(t)

r . Under nominal non-

behavior and should be caused mainly by the presence
of random noise. When this residual does not display a
stationary behavior anymore, this implies a change in the
underlying model describing the behaviour of the WDN.
In this paper, we identify this change as the presence of a
leakage.
Algorithm 1 Leak detection pseudocode
1: for every_timestep : t do

Edge error evaluation
Instead of considering the residual
signal at each node, a novel way to process the residuals
is proposed, whereby the signals are considered on a per-
edge basis, rather than a node one. This stems from the
notion that leaks commonly happen along a pipe rather
than at a junction, and for it being directly applicable
to the benchmark dataset. In the case of an edge euv,
connecting the two neighbouring nodes v and u, we would
consider its residual as r(E)
uv (t) as r(E)
uv (t) = rv(t) −ru(t).
Assess fault condition. Once the edge error is evaluated,
we need to detect possible faulty conditions. The strategy
consists of calculating the moving average of the residual
signal r(E)
uv (t) of a given edge uv in the network, for a
window of size m at observation time t, 1 as per ¯r(E)

is then individually applied on each residual signal at
every time step to obtain a classification of whether the
signal is deemed indicative of a leak being present in its
neighbourhood or not.
1 For the sake of notation simplicity, we omit the dependence on m.

import numpy as np



class FaultDetection():
    def __init__(self, model, threshold):
        self.model = model
        self.threshold = threshold
    
    def fit(self, X_train, Y_train):
        self.model.fit(X_train, Y_train)

    def score(self, X, Y):
        return self.model.score(X, Y)

    def apply_detector(self, X, Y):
        return self.apply_to_labeled_stream(X, Y), None

    def apply_to_labeled_stream(self, X, y):
        y_pred = self.model.predict(X)
        
        return list(np.where(np.abs(y_pred - y) > self.threshold)[0])


def compute_indicator_function(time_points, y_leaks):
    return np.array([1. if t in time_points else 0. for t in range(len(y_leaks))])


class EnsembleSystem():
    def __init__(self, model_class, flow_nodes, pressure_nodes, fault_detecotor_class=FaultDetection):
        self.model_class = model_class
        self.fault_detecotor_class = fault_detecotor_class
        #self.flow_nodes = flow_nodes
        self.pressure_nodes = pressure_nodes    # Only pressure nodes are used!
        self.models = []
    
    def fit(self, X_train, Y_train):    # This is basically a partial_fit
        if len(self.models) == 0:
            self.models = [{"model": self.model_class()} for _ in range(len(self.pressure_nodes))]
        
        for i in range(len(self.pressure_nodes)):
            # Select inputs and output
            inputs_idx = list(range(X_train.shape[1]));inputs_idx.remove(i)
            x_train, y_train = X_train[:,inputs_idx], Y_train[:,i]
        
            # Fit regression
            model = self.models[i]["model"]
            model.fit(x_train, y_train)
            
            # Build fault detector
            max_abs_error = 1.5 * model.get_stat_abs_deviation(x_train, y_train)[0]   # TODO: Magic number
            fault_detector = self.fault_detecotor_class(model, max_abs_error)
            
            # Store model
            self.models[i] = {"model": model, "fault_detector": fault_detector, "input_idx": inputs_idx, "target_idx": i}

    def predict(self, X):
        Y = []
        
        for m in self.models:
            Y.append(m["model"].predict(X[:,m["input_idx"]]))
            
        Y = np.array(Y).T
        return Y
    
    def score(self, X, Y):
        scores = []
        
        for m in self.models:
            scores.append(m["model"].score(X[:,m["input_idx"]], Y[:,m["target_idx"]]))
        
        return scores
    
    def apply_detector(self, X, Y):
        suspicious_time_points = []
        sensor_forecasting_errors = []
        
        for m in self.models:
            x_in = X[:,m["input_idx"]]
            y_truth = Y[:,m["target_idx"]]
            
            sensor_forecasting_errors.append(np.square(m["model"].predict(x_in) - y_truth))# Squared error
            suspicious_time_points += m["fault_detector"].apply_to_labeled_stream(x_in, y_truth)
        
        sensor_forecasting_errors = np.vstack(sensor_forecasting_errors).T
        suspicious_time_points = list(set(suspicious_time_points));suspicious_time_points.sort()   # TODO: Majority voting, weighting, ...?
        return suspicious_time_points, sensor_forecasting_errors
174-        self._indices = range(self.length)

## Reproduce from a fresh clone

Python **3.14** is the tested local environment. The pinned scientific dependencies are in
`requirements.txt`. Raw data are downloaded from the fixed original Zenodo record;
no institutional login is needed for those data.

```bash
git clone https://github.com/Andres-ArangoMartinez/DS675_MiniProject.git
cd DS675_MiniProject
python -m venv .venv
```
Figure 1 is an interpretive supply schematic, not a new hydraulic simulation. Its original
drawing source and nominal-model inputs are identified in the evidence ledger.

Numerical calculations made from public data are not, by themselves, a new learning method.
Research novelty, correct numerical reproduction and authorship are separate questions.

## Engineering-rationale audit added 2026-10-28

The new [prompt audit](PROMPT_VERIFICATION_AUDIT.md), feedback map, engineering discussion,
Ahmed comparison and proposed experiment plan were prepared with Codex assistance. They
are **draft interpretations pending student review**, not invented personal reflections.
The supplied JSON contains attributed rationale, but does not prove that the student
independently executed the nominal/generator checks. The [review log](STUDENT_REVIEW_LOG.md)
therefore leaves personal answers and sign-off blank.

A new static geometry procedure checks official nominal and generator files and records
their checksums, code and outputs in [its own manifest](../results/engineering/audit_manifest.json).
It confirms selected elevations but reports differing pipe distances and tank diameter;
it performs no hydraulic simulation or learned-model training. Earlier manifests describe
their own executions and are not retroactively expanded to cover this new module.

Full-text inspection of the prior ASCE article confirms daily overlays and Average curves
in Figure 4, printed p. 05026021-5. The new user-supplied account connects the earlier
illustrative plot to that research. This supports the shared visual convention, while the
personal inspiration/history remains for student confirmation. See [method transfer](PRIOR_RESEARCH_AND_METHOD_TRANSFER.md).
The copyrighted article/figure is not newly redistributed; the new record contains metadata,
locators and a local full-text hash. Current BattLeDIM curves remain newly computed from
external data, with AI assistance disclosed.

The professor response and group pitch are drafts only. No human approval, group membership,
new regression score, field benefit or blind/untouched 2019 test is asserted.
